In the months that followed, she tried to throw herself back into work, hiking, and organizing her women-in-tech meetup, but her usual rail commute now involved passing the crash site and periodic service disruptions that brought the memories to the surface, and she began quietly rearranging meetings and social plans to avoid peak travel times or certain lines. As her company shifted to a hybrid schedule, she initially welcomed the flexibility but found herself increasingly constrained—turning down speaking invitations in the city, skipping off-Broadway shows with friends, and canceling weekend trips that required train travel—while colleagues noticed her once-energetic presence in cross-functional projects had dulled. After an embarrassing incident where she froze and left a team offsite early when a nearby train crossing alarm malfunctioned and caused a loud screech, her manager gently suggested employee assistance services; Katherine agreed after realizing that more than two years after the derailment, the way it still dominated her reactions and limited her life was no longer something she could \"just power through.\"\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep getting these sudden, vivid memories of the train crash that pop into my head and completely derail whatever I’m doing.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I just lie there wired until two or three in the morning, replaying the day or tensing for some imaginary impact, and when I do finally fall asleep I jerk awake from these train nightmares and never really feel rested enough to think straight at work the next day.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): It’s like my brain hijacks me—I'll be in a Zoom meeting or writing code and suddenly I’m back in that dark, tilted train car hearing people scream and smelling the smoke so vividly that I lose track of what I was saying and have to mute myself to get it together.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve built my whole life around not going near anything train-related—I drive an extra hour in traffic instead of taking Metro-North, I won’t book client meetings in the city if they’re near a station, and I even change the TV channel if there’s a scene on a subway because I can feel my chest tightening just from the sound of it.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What really messes with me is that there are chunks of that hour after the derailment that are just blank—I remember the initial jolt and seeing a woman’s head bleeding next to me, and then it skips ahead to being outside on the gravel, but I can’t fill in how I got from one to the other no matter how hard I try.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the crash, I walk around feeling like the world is basically a rigged game where disaster can hit out of nowhere, and I tell myself I was stupid to ever think I was competent or in control when clearly I can’t even trust something as routine as my commute.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep going over it in my head thinking, 'If I hadn’t stayed late that night,' or 'If I’d noticed something was off and pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): My fuse is ridiculously short now—my boyfriend will be five minutes late or a coworker will ping me with a last-minute request, and I’ll snap and start yelling or firing off a really sharp Slack message, then feel ashamed afterward because I know the reaction is way out of proportion but I can’t seem to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I’m constantly on edge, automatically scanning every room for exits, flinching at loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells, and even in my own apartment I find myself listening for sirens or metal screeches like I’m bracing for something bad to happen again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My whole career and social life have shrunk—I turn down projects or talks that involve traveling by train, leave events early when I feel overwhelmed, and my productivity and reliability at work have dropped enough that my manager has had to pull me aside about it.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty much continuously since the derailment a little over two years ago, instead of easing up like I kept hoping they would.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can In the months that followed, she tried to throw herself back into work, hiking, and organizing her women-in-tech meetup, but her usual rail commute now involved passing the crash site and periodic service disruptions that brought the memories to the surface, and she began quietly rearranging meetings and social plans to avoid peak travel times or certain lines. As her company shifted to a hybrid schedule, she initially welcomed the flexibility but found herself increasingly constrained—turning down speaking invitations in the city, skipping off-Broadway shows with friends, and canceling weekend trips that required train travel—while colleagues noticed her once-energetic presence in cross-functional projects had dulled. After an embarrassing incident where she froze and left a team offsite early when a nearby train crossing alarm malfunctioned and caused a loud screech, her manager gently suggested employee assistance services; Katherine agreed after realizing that more than two years after the derailment, the way it still dominated her reactions and limited her life was no longer something she could \"just power through.\"\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep getting these sudden, vivid memories of the train crash that pop into my head and completely derail whatever I’m doing.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I just lie there wired until two or three in the morning, replaying the day or tensing for some imaginary impact, and when I do finally fall asleep I jerk awake from these train nightmares and never really feel rested enough to think straight at work the next day.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): It’s like my brain hijacks me—I'll be in a Zoom meeting or writing code and suddenly I’m back in that dark, tilted train car hearing people scream and smelling the smoke so vividly that I lose track of what I was saying and have to mute myself to get it together.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve built my whole life around not going near anything train-related—I drive an extra hour in traffic instead of taking Metro-North, I won’t book client meetings in the city if they’re near a station, and I even change the TV channel if there’s a scene on a subway because I can feel my chest tightening just from the sound of it.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What really messes with me is that there are chunks of that hour after the derailment that are just blank—I remember the initial jolt and seeing a woman’s head bleeding next to me, and then it skips ahead to being outside on the gravel, but I can’t fill in how I got from one to the other no matter how hard I try.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the crash, I walk around feeling like the world is basically a rigged game where disaster can hit out of nowhere, and I tell myself I was stupid to ever think I was competent or in control when clearly I can’t even trust something as routine as my commute.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep going over it in my head thinking, 'If I hadn’t stayed late that night,' or 'If I’d noticed something was off and pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): My fuse is ridiculously short now—my boyfriend will be five minutes late or a coworker will ping me with a last-minute request, and I’ll snap and start yelling or firing off a really sharp Slack message, then feel ashamed afterward because I know the reaction is way out of proportion but I can’t seem to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I’m constantly on edge, automatically scanning every room for exits, flinching at loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells, and even in my own apartment I find myself listening for sirens or metal screeches like I’m bracing for something bad to happen again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My whole career and social life have shrunk—I turn down projects or talks that involve traveling by train, leave events early when I feel overwhelmed, and my productivity and reliability at work have dropped enough that my manager has had to pull me aside about it.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty much continuously since the derailment a little over two years ago, instead of easing up like I kept hoping they would.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the In the months that followed, she tried to throw herself back into work, hiking, and organizing her women-in-tech meetup, but her usual rail commute now involved passing the crash site and periodic service disruptions that brought the memories to the surface, and she began quietly rearranging meetings and social plans to avoid peak travel times or certain lines. As her company shifted to a hybrid schedule, she initially welcomed the flexibility but found herself increasingly constrained—turning down speaking invitations in the city, skipping off-Broadway shows with friends, and canceling weekend trips that required train travel—while colleagues noticed her once-energetic presence in cross-functional projects had dulled. pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 a really sharp Slack message, then feel ashamed afterward because I know the reaction is way out of proportion but I can’t seem to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I’m constantly on At the same time, the flashbacks are incredibly disruptive, hijacking my brain while I’m coding or in Zoom meetings and making me completely lose track of what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on edge, easily startled by sudden noises, or do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep due to feeling constantly 'on guard'?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and find myself flinching at sudden sounds like loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells. It makes sleeping almost impossible—I lie awake wired until two or three in the morning bracing for an impact, and when I finally fall asleep, I just jerk awake from train nightmares.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how these feelings of being 'on edge' affect your daily life, such as your ability to concentrate or your relationships with others?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm so exhausted from being constantly on guard and barely sleeping that I can't concentrate or think straight at work. It's also ruining my relationships because my fuse is incredibly short; I'll snap at my boyfriend if he's just five minutes late or fire off really sharp messages to my coworkers, and then I feel deeply ashamed afterward.

It happened a little over two years ago, but it still feels like it's happening right now when those memories hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these vivid memories as a form of flashback, where you feel like you're reliving the train crash, or are they more like recurring, distressing thoughts about the event?\n\nPatient: They definitely feel like actual flashbacks where my brain just hijacks me and I'm suddenly right back in that dark, tilted train car. It's so vivid that I can hear the screaming and smell the smoke, completely losing track of where I am—like during a Zoom meeting where I have to mute myself just to try and pull it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the train crash, or is it mainly these flashbacks that are causing you distress?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely both, and they are both causing me massive distress. I’ve completely restructured my life to avoid anything train-related, like driving an extra hour in traffic to avoid the Metro-North and changing the TV channel if a subway scene comes on. At the same time, the flashbacks are incredibly disruptive, hijacking my brain while I’m coding or in Zoom meetings and making me completely lose track of what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on edge, easily startled by sudden noises, or do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep due to feeling constantly 'on guard'?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and find myself flinching at sudden sounds like loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells. It makes sleeping almost impossible—I lie awake wired until two or three in the morning bracing for an impact, and when I finally fall asleep, I just jerk awake from train nightmares.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how these feelings of being 'on edge' affect your daily life, such as your ability to concentrate or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: I'm so exhausted from being constantly on guard and barely sleeping that I can't concentrate or think straight at work. It's also ruining my relationships because my fuse is incredibly short; I'll snap at my boyfriend if he's just five minutes late or fire off really sharp messages to my coworkers, and then I feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the train crash, or is it more the anxiety and fear that are driving your avoidance and distress?\n\nPatient: I definitely carry a lot of self-blame, constantly replaying that night and thinking if I hadn't stayed late or had pulled the emergency brake, I could have stopped it. I feel so stupid for ever thinking I was in control, but the sheer anxiety and fear of trains are also huge drivers of my daily distress."

In the months that followed, she tried to throw herself back into work, hiking, and organizing her women-in-tech meetup, but her usual rail commute now involved passing the crash site and periodic service disruptions that brought the memories to the surface, and she began quietly rearranging meetings and social plans to avoid peak travel times or certain lines. assistance services; Katherine agreed after realizing that more than two years after the derailment, the way it still dominated her reactions and limited her life was no longer something she could to me, and then it skips ahead to being outside on the gravel, but I can’t fill in how I got from one to the other no matter how hard I try.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the crash, I walk around feeling like the world is basically a rigged game where disaster can hit out of nowhere, and I tell myself I was stupid to ever think I was competent or in control when clearly I can’t even trust something as routine as my commute.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep going over it in my head thinking, 'If I hadn’t stayed late that night,' or 'If I’d noticed something was off and pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 a really sharp Slack message, then feel ashamed afterward because I know the reaction is way out of proportion but I can’t seem to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I’m constantly on edge, automatically scanning every room for exits, flinching at loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells, and even in my own apartment I find myself listening for sirens or metal screeches like I’m bracing for something bad to happen again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My whole career and social life have shrunk—I turn down projects or talks that involve traveling by train, leave events early when I feel overwhelmed, and my productivity and reliability at work have dropped enough that my manager has had to pull me aside about it.\n- duration: These In the months that followed, she tried to throw herself back into work, hiking, and organizing her women-in-tech meetup, but her usual rail commute now involved passing the crash site and periodic service disruptions that brought the memories to the surface, and she began quietly rearranging meetings and social plans to avoid peak travel times or certain lines. As her company shifted to a hybrid schedule, she initially welcomed the flexibility but found herself increasingly constrained—turning down speaking invitations in the city, skipping off-Broadway shows with friends, and canceling weekend trips that required train travel—while colleagues noticed her once-energetic presence in cross-functional projects had dulled. After an embarrassing incident where she froze and left a team offsite early when a nearby train crossing alarm malfunctioned and caused a loud screech, her manager gently suggested employee assistance services; Katherine agreed after realizing that more than two years after the derailment, the way it still dominated her reactions and limited her life was no longer something she could \"just power through.\"\n- Chief Complaint: “I keep getting these sudden, vivid memories of the train crash that pop into my head and completely derail whatever I’m doing.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Zoom meeting or writing code and suddenly I’m back in that dark, tilted train car hearing people scream and smelling the smoke so vividly that I lose track of what I was saying and have to mute myself (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What really messes with me is that there are chunks of that hour after the derailment that are just blank—I remember the initial jolt and seeing a woman’s head bleeding next feeling like the world is basically a rigged game where disaster can hit out of nowhere, and I tell myself I was stupid to ever think I was competent or in control when clearly I can’t even trust something as routine as my commute.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep going over it in my head thinking, 'If I hadn’t stayed late that night,' or 'If I’d noticed something was off and pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): My fuse is ridiculously short now—my boyfriend will be five minutes late or a coworker will ping me with a last-minute request, and I’ll snap and start yelling or firing off Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I keep getting these sudden, vivid memories of the train crash that pop into my head and completely derail whatever I’m doing. It happened a little over two years ago, but it still feels like it's happening right now when those memories hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these vivid memories as a form of flashback, where you feel like you're reliving the train crash, or are they more like recurring, distressing thoughts about the event?\n\nPatient: They definitely feel like actual flashbacks where my brain just hijacks me and I'm suddenly right back in that dark, tilted train car. It's so vivid that I can hear the screaming and smell the smoke, completely losing track of where I am—like during a Zoom meeting where I have to mute myself just to try and pull it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the train crash, or is it mainly these flashbacks that are causing you distress?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely both, and they are both causing me massive distress. I’ve completely restructured my life to avoid anything train-related, like driving an extra hour in traffic to avoid the Metro-North and changing the TV channel if a subway scene comes on. At the same time, the flashbacks are incredibly disruptive, hijacking my brain while I’m coding or in Zoom meetings and making me completely lose track of what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on edge, easily startled by sudden noises, or do you have difficulty falling or staying asleep due to feeling constantly 'on guard'?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and find myself flinching at sudden sounds like loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells. It makes sleeping almost impossible—I lie awake wired until two or three in the morning bracing for an impact, and when I finally fall asleep, I just jerk awake from train nightmares.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how these feelings of being 'on edge' affect your daily life, such as your ability to concentrate or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: I'm so exhausted from being constantly on guard and barely sleeping that I can't concentrate or think straight at work. It's also ruining my relationships because my fuse is incredibly short; I'll snap at my boyfriend if he's just five minutes late or fire off really sharp messages to my coworkers, and then I feel deeply ashamed afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been experiencing feelings of guilt, shame, or self-blame related to the train crash, or is it more the anxiety and fear that are driving your avoidance and distress?\n\nPatient: I definitely carry a lot of self-blame, constantly replaying that night and thinking if I hadn't stayed late or had pulled the emergency brake, I could have stopped it. I feel so stupid for ever thinking I was in control, but the sheer anxiety and fear of trains are also huge drivers of my daily distress.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these flashbacks, do you feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're detached from the world around you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel detached or like I'm watching myself from the outside at all. It actually feels the exact opposite—it's incredibly real, and I get completely pulled back into the terrifying sensory details and physical sensations of the crash itself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're in a daze or spaced out, where things around you seem unreal or distant, especially when you're not having a flashback?"

asleep I jerk awake from these train nightmares and never really feel rested enough to think straight at work the next day.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): It’s like my brain hijacks me—I'll be in a Zoom meeting or writing code and suddenly I’m back in that dark, tilted train car hearing people scream and smelling the smoke so vividly that I lose track of what I was saying and have to mute myself to get it together.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve built my whole life around not going near anything train-related—I drive an extra hour in traffic instead of taking Metro-North, I won’t book client meetings in the city if they’re near a station, and I even change the TV channel if there’s a scene on a subway because I can feel my chest tightening just from the sound of it.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What really messes with me is that there are chunks of that hour after the derailment that are just blank—I remember the initial jolt and seeing a woman’s head bleeding next to me, and then it skips ahead to being outside on the gravel, but I can’t fill in how I got from one to the other no matter how hard I try.\n- S072 (Negative_Beliefs): Since the crash, I walk around feeling like the world is basically a rigged game where disaster can hit out of nowhere, and I tell myself I was stupid to ever think I was competent or in control when clearly I can’t even trust something as routine as my commute.\n- S073 (Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame): I keep going over it in my head thinking, 'If I hadn’t stayed late that night,' or 'If I’d noticed something was off and pulled the emergency brake,' like somehow my being more alert or making a different choice could have prevented the whole thing and kept those people from getting hurt.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): My fuse is ridiculously short now—my boyfriend will be five minutes late or a coworker will ping me with a last-minute request, and I’ll snap and start yelling or firing off a really sharp Slack message, then feel ashamed afterward because I know the reaction is way out of proportion but I can’t seem to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): I’m constantly on edge, automatically scanning every room for exits, flinching at loudspeaker announcements or crossing bells, and even in my own apartment I find myself listening for sirens or metal screeches like I’m bracing for something bad to happen again.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My whole career and social life have shrunk—I turn down projects or talks that involve traveling by train, leave events early when I feel overwhelmed, and my productivity and reliability at work have dropped enough that my manager has had to pull me aside about it.\n- duration: These problems have been hanging over me pretty much continuously since the derailment a little over two years ago, instead of easing up like I kept hoping they would.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.
